# Who scan, era 8 (2026-11-12)

Approved to proceed (coordinator, under Mark's full-auto instruction of 2026-11-12). Read-only scan of era 8 (1650–1815 CE, "The Enlightenment & Awakening Era") of the Church Family Tree under Mark's Who standard. The census, the site and the records were not changed by this scan.

Counts, 411 rows (a paired row counts once): **WHO 137, STORY 51, LISTED 179, WANTED 44**. Judgment-call rows: 232. The live person entry, John Wesley, is WHO, so none is flagged. One WHO row carries the OUTSIDE WITNESS label (Volney). Councils are not people and carry no verdict; leads for the councils list are below.

## How the list was built

- Era 8 has 51 census entries: 50 movements and 1 live person entry (John Wesley). Named voices and the people lists of the movements (old rules), merged by alias.
- Found in text: shapers named in era 8 census long descriptions and sources, and people placed by the corpus-map shelves, marked in the Source tag column.
- By date of main work: the era 7 scan moved people to this era (Lubieniecki, Léger, Morland, Brandt, Lobo, Sebastiani, Vincenzo Maria, Archdeacon Thomas and others); the era 9 scan sent people whose main work is before 1815. Four people the era 9 scan kept (Carey, Richard Allen, Yohannan Hormizd, Gregory V of Constantinople) are not scored here.
- Seven drafting workers (Sonnet) sorted the candidates by region and opened the cited lines; two more drafted the carried rows. One Opus subagent then opened the cited lines for every WHO, STORY and WANTED row, spot-checked LISTED rows, and re-tested every WANTED row against the era 7 precedents (`Opus_Check_Notes.md`).
- Editions tagged "[suggested; to confirm]" or "[recollection; to confirm]" were named from memory, not from the census or the Library. The Library thread confirms them.

## Rulings applied

- The standard of eras 1 to 7 (Decision-Log 2026-11-11 and 2026-11-12): WHO is church-shaping with a body of work or a following and enough life to tell; recorders are WHO with a thin own life flagged; women are WHO if they founded, taught or led; rulers and popes are STORY unless they have their own voice; founders known through enemies need two witnesses for the teaching; OUTSIDE WITNESS is a label for objective historians outside the church, never partisans or polemicists, and a writer from a rival church is a hostile inside writer.
- Pairing: "if two people are tied together in their sources with similar identity its ok to make that a single who". Paired rows are judgment calls.
- A later retelling alone gives LISTED until an early source is held. Famous writers with almost no recoverable life are LISTED. A story-type person whose own life-telling book is not held is WANTED, and the source must be able to carry a life.
- Era 7 precedents from the coordinator (2026-11-12): someone whose own work is held and whose life can be told from that work plus a following or held witnesses is WHO, not WANTED; a founder with no text of his own is WHO if two independent held witnesses carry him; one held source that shows a woman shaping the church makes her WHO.
- Era is by date of main work. A person whose life straddles a boundary is scored by the date of the main work and flagged.
- "Major?" column (proposal only): the map shows up to 4 names per tradition and 5 per river per era, chosen from the major Whos. Mark confirms it in each batch.

## Questions and the coordinator's answers

New judgment calls only. The answers are coordinator calls under Mark's full-auto instruction of 2026-11-12. None changes the standard; each applies a settled precedent. Mark may overrule.

1. Heads of churches and parties with no text of their own held: WHO, STORY or LISTED? Hacob IV of Julfa, Simeon of Erevan, Mar Thoma I, Mar Dionysius I and Theodore de Cock are WHO in the table. Each led a church or party and has a following and a life told by two witnesses, but no book of theirs is held. Rulers are STORY unless they have their own voice, and church heads are not rulers.
   - (a) WHO, flagged "no own text held" (recommended). It follows the founder rule (two independent held witnesses carry the person) and keeps the Armenian and Malabar lines in view.
   - (b) STORY, as with rulers who have no voice of their own.
   - (c) LISTED until a text of theirs is held.
   - **Answer.** Coordinator call: apply the two-witness precedent (Ferenc Dávid, de Nobili). WHO, flagged "no own text held", where two independent held witnesses carry the person; STORY where only one does. All five went under the two-witness rule and stay WHO: Hacob IV (Chamich and Ormanian), Simeon of Erevan (Ormanian and Chamich), Mar Thoma I (the Malankara history and the Roman Carmelite sources), Mar Dionysius I (Buchanan and Paulinus) and De Cock (Hoynck, the Acta and Neale).

2. Compilers: how far does the recorder rule go? Freylinghausen (the Halle hymnbook) and Reitz (the Wiedergebohrnen collection) are WHO as compilers with a thin own life. The A1 drafter put compilers of one person's life (Moore for Mary Fletcher, Hopkins for Osborn, Thomas Jones for Charles) at LISTED. The two groups used the rule differently.
   - (a) One rule: a compiler is WHO only where the collection is the tradition's own voice and a life of the compiler is told in a held line; compilers of one person's life are LISTED. Freylinghausen stays WHO; Reitz moves to LISTED (recommended).
   - (b) Keep both as the table has them (hymn compilers WHO, single-life compilers LISTED).
   - (c) All compilers LISTED; the books are served as works.
   - **Answer.** Coordinator call: option (a). Freylinghausen stays WHO; Reitz goes to LISTED (his own life is told in no held line).

3. Antoine Arnauld and Pasquier Quesnel. The check moved both from WANTED to WHO under the era 7 precedents. Arnauld rests on two witnesses (Pascal's letters of 1656 and the nuns' narratives) and no work of his own is held, so the founder precedent is stretched to the movement's theologian. Quesnel's own words are held only as the 101 propositions in the bull; his life comes from Neale (1858), a later retelling.
   - (a) Arnauld WHO, flagged; Quesnel LISTED, because a later retelling alone gives LISTED until an early source is held (recommended).
   - (b) Both WHO, as the table has them.
   - (c) Both back to WANTED, naming Arnauld's Œuvres (1775-83) and Quesnel's Réflexions morales.
   - **Answer.** Coordinator call: Arnauld is WHO, flagged. Quesnel by the Melanchthon rule exactly. The bull Unigenitus of 1713 is held (UNIG), but it prints only the 101 condemned propositions, not the Réflexions morales, and his life rests on Neale 1858 alone. So Quesnel is LISTED, under the later-retelling rule; he is WHO if the Réflexions are vendored.

4. The OUTSIDE WITNESS label for travellers. Volney (an Enlightenment philosopher) and Ludolf (a German scholar outside every Ethiopian party) carry it. Rycaut (an Anglican diplomat on the Greek and Armenian churches) and Tournefort (a French botanist, a Catholic) are travellers, not historians, and members of rival churches. Moreau de Saint-Méry was relabelled as a plain recorder under the settled rule (a partisan), as a coordinator call.
   - (a) Keep the label for Volney and Ludolf only; Rycaut and Tournefort become plain WHO recorders (recommended).
   - (b) Keep all four, with the bias marked in each profile.
   - (c) Label only Ludolf, the one historian.
   - **Answer.** Coordinator call: follow the era 7 Herberstein precedent (a Christian writer is inside Christianity, whatever the church). Rycaut (Anglican), Tournefort (Catholic) and Ludolf (Lutheran) are plain WHO recorders with no label. Volney is the only label kept, with his skepticism marked as his standpoint. Moreau de Saint-Méry is also plain, under the settled rule on partisans.

5. People whose era 8 work has no era 8 census entry. Lubieniecki, Léger, Morland, Brandt and Lobo belong to an era 7 or era 9 census entry, and Absalom Jones, McGready, Thomas Campbell and Hauge sit on an era 9 shelf. They are scored here by date of main work, so the map has no place to show them.
   - (a) Score here by date of work and register the census gaps in Open-Gaps (Polish Brethren in exile, Waldensians after 1655, Remonstrant brotherhood, Black churches of the new republic) for a later census change (recommended).
   - (b) Score them at their own census entry's era with an era 8 date.
   - (c) Drop them from the era 8 table until an era 8 census entry exists.
   - **Answer.** Coordinator call: option (a). Score here by date of work and register the census gaps in Open-Gaps.

It found no invented person-detail. Second pass: the 23 rows added later, 21 confirmed and 2 corrected; no live row remains for the four people sent to era 9; every WANTED row re-tested against the era 7 precedents. All changes are applied.

## Coordinator calls under settled rules, made by the scan

Not Mark's words. Each is a call under a ruling already made; Mark may overrule. The drafting workers' own notes, by region.

**English-speaking revivals: Quakers, Methodists, New England, Wales, Shakers, New Sweden**

- Whitefield: one row, WHO, named in the Methodist Revival and the Evangelical Awakening in New England. He is counted under the Methodist four on the map and appears in New England by the same row.
- John Wesley (live person entry): comes out WHO. No live person entry comes down, so none is flagged.
- Pair rule: Evans and Cheevers (one book, same role) and Meacham with Wright (one voice in the census, joint offices) are single rows and judgment calls. Kept apart: Osborn and Anthony (different roles in the Newport society); Fox and Fell (different works and roles); Ann Lee and Whittaker and William Lee (different generation and office); Charles and John Wesley (two separate bodies of work).
- Women rule: Fell, Mary Fletcher, Ann Lee, Wright, Rogers and Osborn are WHO because they founded, led or taught. Evans and Cheevers, Ashbridge, Mary Penington, Anthony and Sarah Edwards are STORY, because the held record is one testimony and no teaching or leadership. Ann Griffiths is a separate question (Question 4).
- Black and enslaved preachers: Allen is WHO (founded and led, own autobiography). Wheatley is STORY (testimony, not a preacher). Occom, Haynes and Hammon are WANTED or LISTED because the Library holds no life-telling source. Hosier is LISTED, with no early source that could carry a life.
- Founders known through followers and an enemy (Ann Lee, Whittaker): the teaching has two witnesses (the Society's Testimonies and Brown's ex-member account); the earliest hostile pamphlets are not held. Brown himself is a hostile inside writer, WHO with the polemic marked.
- Recorder WHO with a thin own life: Acrelius, Jesse Lee and McNemar. Compilers of a single life are LISTED under Question 3.
- No OUTSIDE WITNESS rows: no Enlightenment, secular, Jewish, Muslim or Asian historian is held on these shelves. The corpus-map calls Acrelius and Asbury "outside witness" on the Quaker shelf; under the era 8 rule they are writers from a rival church, not outside witnesses (Open-Gaps).
- Era 8 date rule: main work 1650 or later. Fox's first call (1647) is before 1650; his main work is after. Allen, Asbury and Jesse Lee die in 1816 or 1831 but their main work falls in the window.
- Antecedent-role authors on my shelves (Law, Watts, Francke, Bengel, Zinzendorf, Arminius) are not scored here; see Not scored.

**Baptists, Old Dissent, Unitarians and Universalists, Church of England, Swedenborgians, Mennonites, the Cape**

- Doctrine and devotion writers whose files carry no life (Butler, Law, Keach, Secker, Chauncy, Mayhew, Gay, Ballou, Barlow, Moss) are LISTED on the story test (Hilton precedent, era 6). Where a named contemporary or own life-telling source exists and the person clearly shaped the church (Watts, Doddridge, Fuller, Bray, Matthew Henry, Wilberforce, Hannah More), WANTED. Later retellings alone give LISTED (Winchester, Ballou, Law, Sancroft). See Question 1.
- Liele and David George are kept apart, though the census puts them in one voice. Each has a separate first-person account in Rippon (RIP 26715 on; RIP 37340 on), different churches and different years. A single row would hide that each tells his own life. Judgment call; they could be paired if Mark prefers.
- Priestley, Lindsey and Bunyan: one row each. Priestley is named in four entries, Lindsey in two (plus a needs-ruling corpus-map row in Old Dissent), Bunyan in two.
- John and Judith Murray kept apart: husband and wife but different callings (preacher and essayist), and only John has a held life.
- Swedenborg is WHO although he founded nothing; the church rests on his books and his autobiographical letter (SWED-HH 2473 on). Marked judgment call.
- Recorders: van Braght, Terrill and Belsham are WHO with a thin own life flagged. Rippon, Morgan Edwards and Humphreys are LISTED because nothing in their files tells their lives (Question 2).
- Founders known through enemies: none among the WHO rows. Priestley, Lindsey and Murray all speak for themselves.
- Outside witnesses: none. Enlightenment, secular, Jewish and Muslim historians are not named in these eight entries. Morgan Edwards (EDW-M 8) calls himself both an inside witness for Baptists and an outside witness for the Brethren; he is LISTED here, so no label is applied. Rimius (an anti-Moravian polemicist, RIM in the corpus-map) is a hostile writer, not an outside witness.
- Hostile inside writers: Barrington and Ridgley are opposed Salters' Hall pamphleteers; both are LISTED. Barlow's Brutum Fulmen is polemic against Rome; LISTED.
- Era boundary cases scored in era 8: Baxter (Saints' Rest 1650; main work 1650-1691), Bunyan (works 1660-1688; the boyhood is before 1650), Terrill (church gathered 1640, his own records 1658-1685), Biddle (works 1647-1654 straddle; entered through the 1653 translation).
- Women: Agnes Beaumont is STORY (testimony, no founding or leading). Judith Sargent Murray, Anne Dutton and Hannah More are WANTED under the women rule, since each wrote or taught and a life source would settle them.
- Namesake traps are listed under Not scored.

**Pietists, Brethren, Moravians, Hungarian and Transylvanian Protestants, Utrecht**

- Persons tied by one act kept as one row (judgment calls, flagged): Ziegenbalg and Plütschau; Dober and Nitschmann; Beké and Járai; Mártonffi and Steinville; Leopold I and Charles VI. Kept apart: Johann Wilhelm Petersen and Johanna Eleonora Petersen (her own Life is a separate, unheld book); Steenoven and Varlet (consecrated and consecrator, with different sources); Spangenberg and Zinzendorf; Mack and Hochmann (teacher and friend, each with his own text); Christian David and Dober (founding of the settlement versus the first mission).
- Compilers (Freylinghausen's hymnbook, Reitz's lives) are WHO with a thin own life, under the recorder rule; question 1 asks whether Mark agrees.
- Church-historian recorders of the Moravians (Cranz, Loskiel, Oldendorp, La Trobe) are WHO with thin own lives. All four are marked "no" for the map: none shaped the church beyond the record.
- Rulers: Joseph II, Leopold I and Charles VI are LISTED (not STORY) because no text of theirs is held, which is what separated STORY from LISTED for the era 6 rulers.
- Hostile inside writers: Rimius, Acrelius (on the Moravians) and Hoynck van Papendrecht are LISTED because no life of them is told in a held line; Sauer's pamphlet is read as polemic, but Sauer has a life and a press, so he is WHO. No OUTSIDE WITNESS label is used in this group: no secular, Jewish, Muslim or Asian historian is held for these six entries.
- József Ferencz, János Kriza, Pál Augustinovics, Sándor Bölöni Farkas and Thomas Rees are era 9 by date of main work (see overlap section). Ferencz's 1875 book and the 1907 Short Account compiled under him are used here only as late retellings of 1660-1815 events.
- Pius VI and Clement XI are not scored here: their texts are context for the Utrecht map, but their home is the Port-Royal or ancien-régime entries; the coordinator should confirm which group scores them.
- Entry with no corpus-map file: hungarian-and-transylvanian-protestantism-1650-1815 has no corpus-map yaml, and both of its named sources (Bauhofer 1854 and Aldasy's Catholic Encyclopedia article) are not in cic/texts. No WHO, STORY or WANTED can be sorted from this entry; its persons are LISTED.
- Roses of Saron, the pastors sold to the Naples galleys, the Polish Unitarian exiles of 1660 and the Czech refugees of 1722 are groups: not scored.

**Port-Royal, Quietists, Camisards, Huguenots, the French and Roman Catholic church, the Jesuits**

- Muratori as an outside witness, decided: no. He is a Catholic priest, inside the church, and the census says he "never saw Paraguay", drew on Jesuit letters and "praises the missions". That is a secondhand friendly compiler, not an objective historian outside the church. Verdict WANTED (his one held book carries no life).
- No OUTSIDE WITNESS WHO for these six entries. The Library holds no Enlightenment, secular, Jewish or Muslim writer for them (checked the file list for Voltaire, Rulhière, Gibbon, Sainte-Beuve, Michelet and similar: none). Burnet (Anglican bishop, Letter I on Molinos) is a rival-church writer and a traveller, not an outside witness; see Not scored.
- Pairs kept as one row: Fontaine and du Fossé (WANTED, memoirists printed together); Innocent X and Alexander VII (STORY, one condemnation and its Formulary). Clement XIV and Pius VII are one row each across two entries, as ordered.
- Pairs kept apart: Molinos and Guyon (different lives, countries, works, and one is a priest, the other a widow); Fénelon and Bossuet (opposite sides, each with held works); the three Angéliques and Agnès (Mère Angélique, Agnès her sister, Angélique de Saint-Jean her niece: separate works and decades); Rabaut father and son (a hidden pastor against a public deputy and writer, each with a separate record); Lancelot kept apart from Fontaine and du Fossé (schoolmaster, with a held text of his own).
- Popes with only official texts: STORY (Clement XI, Clement XIV, Pius VI, Pius VII, Innocent XI, Innocent XII, Innocent X and Alexander VII, Benedict XIV). Pontificate dates are not read in held lines and are tagged as recollection where given.
- Rulers with official texts on the shelf: STORY (Louis XIV, Louis XVI). Other rulers named as forces: Question 2.
- Hostile inside writers (Brueys, Louvreleul, Fléchier, Barruel): LISTED, because no life can be told from the held lines. The Gui precedent would allow WHO if the introductions told a life.
- Recorders: Fontaine and du Fossé WANTED (the memoirs are the missing source); Sister Marie of Compiègne STORY (an eyewitness testimony, not a church recorder).
- Marie Durand: WANTED, on the Kempe precedent, with STORY as the alternative (judgment call). Isabeau Vincent: STORY on a single hostile witness (the life may rest on one).
- Rows scored although the main work straddles era 7: Mère Angélique (see Question 1) and Marie de l'Incarnation of Quebec. Jansen and Saint-Cyran are not scored (Flagged).
- The Port-Royal entry's old-rules list had no entry for Antoine Arnauld or Jansen, only the voices. Arnauld is WANTED, Jansen is flagged for era 7.
- Placement notes: Challoner, Laval, Marie of Quebec, Lawrence and Montfort are WHO on the strength of the corpus-map shelf, flagged provisional by the Library itself. The census text of the old-regime entry names none of them.
- Pius VI's death at Valence: the Library holds two eyewitness books (Baldassari 1839, Merck 1814). Merck is scored (STORY); Baldassari is flagged for era 9 by date of print.

**Old Believers, the Russian and Ottoman Orthodox, Georgia, Ruthenian and Romanian Greek Catholics**

- Nikon (the named ruler-type): own voice plus influence on the church. WHO chosen; the Replies are his reasoning on the priesthood against the Crown, and the Old Believers' whole case is against his acts. Marked judgment call. One row for both entries (Old Believers and Russian Church).
- Peter the Great (ruler rule): STORY, judgment call. His own voice is a war journal; the church texts in his name were drafted by Prokopovich.
- Catherine II, Maria Theresa and Joseph II (rulers): STORY by the ruler rule, with only a late secondary (Pelesz 1880) held; none has a held text of her own. If Mark prefers no ruler without a held text of their own, these three drop to LISTED.
- Morozova under the women rule: STORY. She sheltered, supported and suffered; no teaching of her own is held.
- Pairs made: Andrei and Semyon Denisov; Nicodemus and Macarius of Corinth; Macarius III Zaim and Paul of Aleppo; Maria Theresa and Joseph II; Urusova and Danilova. All are judgment calls. Kept apart: Mitrofan and Paisius Velichkovsky (disciple-recorder and teacher; Mitrofan's own text is not held apart from the brotherhood's Life); Filippov and the Denisovs (a later abbot who recorded them); Avvakum and Morozova (teacher and patron); Antoni I and Antoni II (namesakes); Lazar, Fyodor and Epifanii (each separate, and the first two disagreed with Avvakum).
- Recorders under the recorder rule: Filippov, Dapontes, Makraios, Basilovits, Kishka (as editor and Life-writer of Pociej), Ligarides (hostile; polemic marked), Paul of Aleppo. Thin own life flagged in each row.
- Hostile inside writers: Ligarides (the prosecutor of Nikon, WHO as recorder with polemic marked, Gui precedent); Covel (title shows polemic, LISTED).
- Later retelling alone gives LISTED: Stefan Yavorsky (Mouravieff 1842 only), Platon (no life at all).
- WANTED confirmed by search (cic/texts and file list): Nicodemus and Macarius (no Life), Tikhon of Zadonsk (counsels only), Herman of Alaska (no Alaska text), Klein (no Romanian Greek Catholic text).
- Namesake traps noted: three Paisios (Velichkovsky, Ligarides, of Alexandria) and Paisii of Hilendar; Nikita Dobrynin against Nikon; Joseph II of Austria against Metropolitan Joseph II Soltan; Szeptycki against the 20th-century Sheptytsky; the two Antonis of Georgia; Dositheus of Jerusalem (Notaras) against Dositheos, archbishop of Telavi and the Caucasus (BROS2 18325-18329).
- Georgian entry: the census gives Macrina as sister of King Teimuraz I. Brosset shows her as sister of King David, who died in 1722. Not changed here; logged for the Library label (Open-Gaps).
- Date rule: Gregory V scored in era 8 by main work (1797) and flagged for era 9 (death 1821); Walsh (1836) is a source file, not scored as a person; Curzon (1849) and Ioselian (1866) are era 9.
- Council participants: Nikon, Avvakum, Neronov, Lazar, Ligarides, Paisios of Alexandria, Macarius III (Great Moscow Synod 1666-67); Dositheus, Nectarius (Synod of Jerusalem 1672); Kishka, Szeptycki, Hrebnicki, Grimaldi (Synod of Zamość 1720). Named in the rows. No new council table.

**Armenians, Melkites, Copts, Ethiopia, Church of the East, St Thomas Christians, Balkan Catholics**

- Pairs: none made. Kept apart: Macarius III and his son Paul of Aleppo (different role, different book); Hormizd, his uncle Elia and his cousin Yeshua-yau; Mar Thoma I and Mar Gregorius; Cyril V Zaim and Cyril VI Tanas; Athanasius IV Dabbas and Sylvester (rivals, not a pair).
- Church heads without a held text of their own (Hacob IV, Simeon of Erevan, Mar Thoma I, Mar Dionysius I) are WHO as heads with a following and a life told by two witnesses. This is the reading of the settled rule, not a new category; see question 3.
- Recorders as WHO with a thin own life: Zakaria of Kanaker and Paul of Aleppo.
- Hostile inside writers kept LISTED because no life can be told from the held lines: Serpos, Paulinus, Raulin and Geddes.
- First-person testimony of a non-shaper: Abba Gregorius and Mar Gabriel as STORY. Church heads without own words and with a violent end: Avedik STORY; a priest martyr with no body of work: Gomidas STORY.
- A late retelling alone: Athanasius the Copt and Ibrahim al-Jawhari rest on Butcher (1897, not held). Athanasius is LISTED (no early source named); Ibrahim is WANTED because al-Jabarti is a plausible early source [suggested; to confirm].
- Outside witnesses: Ludolf, Volney and Rycaut carry the label as judgment calls; none is a partisan. Bruce would carry it if held (WANTED). Buchanan does not carry it (a missionary advocate). Tournefort and Chardin are LISTED (plain travellers; question 1).
- Conflicting dates are marked Contested in the Dates column: Athanasius IV's death (24 July or 5 August 1724), Mar Gabriel's arrival and death, Ahatallah's fate, Mar Dionysius I's number and death, Hacob IV's deathbed submission.
- Council participation named in rows: Macarius III (Great Moscow Synod, 1666-1667, already on the councils list); Cyril VI (the national synod of 25 April 1730 is a lead below).
- Source of the Gomidas quotation in the census ("I am a Christian and a follower of St. Gregory the Illuminator, Armenian by birth") was not found in NUR-E, CHAMICH or ORMANIAN; row cites what is found.

**Missions: Kongo, Latin America, Philippines, China, Vietnam, Ceylon, the VOC world**

- Kongo hostile inside writers: Cavazzi is WHO (Gui precedent, polemic marked as polemic); his judgments of Kongo religion and of Njinga are the friars' view. Da Gallo and Lorenzo da Lucca are not held, so da Gallo is WANTED, Lorenzo da Lucca LISTED.
- Kimpa Vita is a founder known through enemies. The two-witness test would be met by da Gallo and Lorenzo da Lucca as two authors, though both are Capuchin partisans. Neither is held, so the verdict is WANTED, not LISTED. If only one relation were acquired, she would stay WANTED until the second one is held.
- Njinga is STORY, not WHO: a ruler without a held voice. Her life has two independent witnesses in the Library (Cavazzi; Cadornega via Paiva) plus the pope's letter.
- Mission narratives (Carli and Guattini, Merolla, Zucchelli) are first-person testimonies by non-shapers: STORY, with Carli and Guattini as one paired row. Merolla and Zucchelli kept apart from them and from each other (different decades, different parts of the kingdom).
- Recorders are WHO only where the held text shows a life: Palóu (named office, signs, quoted by Serra) is WHO; Montalvo, García de la Concepción, Hansen, Toro, Calleja, Florencia and Murillo Velarde are LISTED because the held volume carries no life of the writer beyond a title page.
- Popes with official texts are STORY where the held text shows an episode (Benedict XIV, Alexander VII); Clement X and Innocent XI are LISTED because their bulls are held only inside another writer's book.
- Women: Sor Juana WHO (taught by the pen, Julian precedent); Úrsula de Jesús and Njinga are not Whos on the record.
- Sor Juana's birth year is Contested (Calleja gives 12 Nov 1651; later scholarship prefers 1648 [recollection; to confirm]). The row follows the held text and says so.
- Era by main work: Vieira and Rhodes are scored here (main work after 1650); Rose of Lima, Martín de Porres, Juan Diego and Mariana de Quito go to era 7.
- Japan's hidden Christians: both census voices (Petitjean and Domingo) are events of 1865, so era 9 by the settled ruling; nothing in this entry is scored for era 8.
- Aquino de Belén: LISTED because the record has no life (a town, a title, an office as printer); see Question 2.
- Bernardo da Gallo, Kimpa Vita and Pedro IV all rest on one wanted pair of Capuchin relations; the Open-Gaps entry is one item with three rows behind it.
- Namesake traps: Charles Maigrot (bishop of Conon, Fujian) versus Jean-Baptiste Maigrot (procurator at Macao, vicar apostolic of Sichuan); João Ferreira de Almeida versus Manuel de Almeida (Jesuit, Ethiopia history); Njinga versus Mvemba a Nzinga (Afonso I of Kongo, in the PAIVA header); the "Lovers of the Cross" of Tonkin versus the Philippine beaterios (BR48 3880-3925 is the Tonkin dispute, not the Manila houses).
- Persons in two of my entries: Pallu and Lambert de la Motte (Chinese and Vietnamese entries): one paired row. Benedict XIV (Chinese and colonial Latin America), Clement X (colonial and Vietnamese) and Innocent XI (colonial and Chinese): one row each.
- Other groups' entries may score Vieira, Rhodes, Mailla and Benedict XIV (the Society of Jesus 1650-1814 and the Ancien Régime entries). Merge by name at the table stage.
- Not scored as editors or later compilers of held volumes: Castorena y Ursúa (editor of Sor Juana's third volume), Juarros (1809 chronicler of Guatemala), Cadornega (extract in Paiva), Alamandini (arranger of Cavazzi), Piccardo (writer-up of Merolla), Faber (1855 editor of the Life of Rose), Blair and Robertson.

**Carried from the era 7 scan**

- Archdeacon Thomas: WHO on the founders-through-enemies rule (two independent witnesses: a hostile Carmelite eyewitness and the community's own history). The era 7 worker's WANTED proposal is not followed. Flagged for Mark as question 1.
- Sebastiani: WHO as a hostile inside writer with a life that can be told (Gui precedent). His polemic is marked in the row. Not an outside witness.
- Vincenzo Maria: STORY under the story rule (first-person testimony by a non-shaper). Kept apart from Sebastiani, not paired: their roles differ (leader and bishop versus companion and traveller), and each wrote a separate book. If Mark prefers pairing, the combined row would be a judgment call under the pairing rule.
- Léger: WHO as a moderator and chief historian of his own church. Inside writer, not rival-church, so the Gui precedent does not apply in the same way; the contested documents in Book I and the disputed death toll are marked.
- Morland: STORY. He did not shape the church; he rallied outside support. Not WHO as recorder because his own record is about the English side, and not an outside witness because he was a Protestant partisan.
- Lubieniecki and Brandt: WHO as recorders with the life flagged (Lubieniecki's life is told fully by his son; Brandt's is a ministry roster). Both are inside writers for minority churches, not rivals.
- Lobo: STORY. A missionary of the losing mission, so he did not shape the Ethiopian church. Scored once, with the main mission in era 7 flagged below.
- Not WANTED: no person here needed a missing source. Lobo's Portuguese Itinerário and Brandt's volumes 1, 2 and 4 are gaps in editions, not missing lives (see Open-Gaps items).
- Date rule: all eight are scored in era 8 by date of main work or text (printing or writing 1658 to 1685), as the task asked. Where the life straddles the line it is flagged below.
- Pairs kept apart: Sebastiani and Vincenzo Maria (above); Léger and Morland (both Waldensian advocacy, but Léger is an insider and Morland an English envoy); Archdeacon Thomas and Sebastiani (opponents, not a pair).

**Sent from the era 9 scan**

- Raikes and Stock: one pair row, WANTED. Not held: grep of cic/texts finds Raikes only in editor's footnotes (WES7) and as namesakes (WES2 15598 is Raikes the elder, the newspaper's founder; CHEAP-REPO subscriber lists name Thomas and William Raikes). Stock appears nowhere.
- Absalom Jones: WHO although no solo text is held. The Narrative of 1793-94 is his own jointly signed text, and ALLEN carries his life from a partner. Marked judgment call because the life rests on one witness.
- McGready: WANTED, not LISTED. He started the Logan County revival and his own narrative is a first-person account. Asbury (ASB3 2140, 2439) only names him; MCN never does.
- Thomas Campbell: scored as era 8 by the date of the Declaration and Address (1809), though the census shelf is era 9. Flagged below.
- Hauge: scored as era 8 by the date of his books (1796-1804) and mission. Census shelf is era 9. Flagged below.
- Universalist hymnal men: one group row, LISTED, because no one of the five has a tellable life in a held line. Hosea Ballou is already scored (Early American Unitarians and Universalists, table row; LISTED). Edward Turner and Zebulon Streeter are named in the 1903 centennial book as leaders; that is a later retelling, so LISTED stands until an early source (Stacy 1850) is held.
- William Wells Jr.: kept apart from James Freeman. The Freeman row does not mention him, and one letter with a publishing credit is thinner than Freeman's record.
- Georg Schmidt: not rescored. Already in the table.
- Kosmas Aitolos and Athanasios Parios: WANTED by the Margery Kempe precedent for a life-and-teaching figure whose record is not held. For Athanasios the fallback to LISTED is stated.
- Assemani: the senior and junior are kept apart, not paired. The brief's "Giuseppe Simone and Joseph" turned out to be the same man under Italian and English names, with the nephew as a distinct person in the Collectanea.
- Balaam: LISTED, scored here with a flag because the brief asked for a decision. Almost all of his work falls after 1811 and into the era 9 reach of the Russian-Georgian church.

No text of his own is held; only letters quoted by Neale. Three held witnesses from both parties (Hoynck for Rome, the Acta and Neale for Utrecht) tell the same life: Propaganda pupil, Leyden pastor, Rome envoy of the chapter in 1688, then Rome's man who replaced Codde. Utrecht's side is polemic: Neale says he "had given in to its adversaries". Marked as polemic. Namesakes: Hendrik de Cock (1801-1842, Secession of 1834, era 9); Andrew de Cock, a martyr in Neale 7005. The brief's "archbishop of Utrecht (1688)" is wrong: he was never archbishop; 1688 is his journey to Rome. ## Leads for the councils list

Councils, synods, assemblies and summits found along the way and missing from the councils list (`Build/Ministry/Operations/Audits/2026-11-12_major-events-scan/Councils_Scan.md`). Not scored.

**English-speaking revivals: Quakers, Methodists, New England, Wales, Shakers, New Sweden**

None of the seven entries has a council on the Councils_Scan era 8 list. Conferences and assemblies in my entries' texts that are not on it:

- Christmas Conference, Baltimore, 24 Dec 1784 to about 2 Jan 1785 (the organising conference of the Methodist Episcopal Church). HELD: COKE 1003-1004 ("On Christmas Eve we opened our Conference, which has continued ten days"); LEEJ 3155-3279; MIN (Minutes) cover the American conferences. Takers: Coke, Asbury.
- Methodist annual Conference, first held in London, 25 to 27 June 1744. HELD: MIN 239 ("London, June 25-27, 1744"); LARGE 1 and 59 (Wesley's Minutes of Several Conversations). A recurring body, listed once.
- First Calvinistic Methodist Association, Watford, Glamorgan, 5 and 6 January 1743 (OCR of year in HUGH 6688). HELD: HUGH 6686-6690; the minutes of the Watford association of 1743-1744 are printed in Hughes (HUGH 9). Participants: Harris, Rowland, Whitefield (Whitefield's presidency is from my recollection, not a held line: to confirm).
- London Yearly Meeting of Friends (recurring from 1675 epistles) and Philadelphia Yearly Meeting (first held at Burlington, 1681). HELD: LYM 474 (Epistle 1, 1675); LYM 60; PYM 1 and 31. Recurring bodies, listed once each.

**Baptists, Old Dissent, Unitarians and Universalists, Church of England, Swedenborgians, Mennonites, the Cape**

Known era 8 rows used for naming only: General Baptist Assembly (1654-1811, HELD), Synod of Dort (1618-1619, era 7).

Missing from the Councils_Scan list (name, date, Library file if held):
- Savoy Conference, 1661, London. HELD in part: BAX 10123-10124 and 10390 ("our dispute at the Savoy ended"); the Baxter Reliquiae of 1696 (baxter_reliquiae-baxterianae_sylvester1696) also holds the conference papers with the bishops. Involves Anglicans and Presbyterians (census: restoration-georgian-church-of-england, english-old-dissent).
- Salters' Hall Meeting, 1719, London. HELD: calamy_historical-account-of-my-own-life-v2 (contents CAL 360-365); barrington-john-shute_account-of-late-proceedings-at-salters-hall_1719; ridgley-thomas_unreasonableness-of-the-charge-of-imposition_1719; general-baptist-assembly minutes v2. Named in english-old-dissent and in the Baptist and Unitarian lines. Presbyterians, Congregationalists and Baptists: probably river-level (Protestant).
- Exeter Assembly (Peirce case), 1718-1719. Named in CAL 360-365; the meeting's records are not held.
- Savoy Assembly (Congregational Declaration of Faith), 1658. Not held. (Savoy Declaration is a Congregational statement; census-adjacent.)
- First General Conference of the New Church, Great East Cheap, 13 April 1789. HELD: new-jerusalem-church_minutes-of-the-first-general-conference_hindmarsh1789 (MIN 1056-1062, 1784-1790; 42 propositions). Participants include Thomas Wright (President), Robert Hindmarsh (Treasurer and Secretary), Henry Peckitt, Robert Beatson, August Nordenskiold, Charles Berns Wadstrom, Samuel Hands.
- Winchester Convention of the Universalists, 1803. PARTLY HELD: universalist-church_winchester-centennial-with-the-profession-of-belief_1903 (1903 retelling; no 1803 printing found).
- Convention of the Protestant Episcopal Church at Philadelphia, 1785-1787 (Freeman's ordination referred to it, BELS 570-580). Records not held.
- Philadelphia Baptist Association, from 1707 (EDW-M 257-289, 403: "formed themselves into an association"). HELD in part: Morgan Edwards 1770; Keach's catechism keyed to its adoption.
- Ministers' meeting at Kettering, 2 Oct 1792 (founding of the Baptist Missionary Society). RIP 29322-29335. An assembly founding a society; judgment: an event, not a council.

**Pietists, Brethren, Moravians, Hungarian and Transylvanian Protestants, Utrecht**

- Synods of the Moravian Church (not on the Councils_Scan era 8 list): Gotha, June 1740 (SPANG 14250-14256); the General Synod at Marienborn, c.1740-41 (PERIOD 3571, "present at the General Synod at Marienborn"); the Synod at Zeist, 1746 (CRANZ 1173, contents); the General Synods of 1764 (PERIOD 5844; CRANZ 1197) and 1769 (CRANZ 1197); the Synod at Herrnhut, 1789 (PERIOD 7483-7484, "the synod of the Brethren, met at Herrnhut in 1789"; the census names it). Library files: spangenberg_life-of-zinzendorf_jackson1838, moravian-church_periodical-accounts-missions-v1_1790, cranz_ancient-and-modern-history-of-the-brethren_latrobe1780. Participants: Zinzendorf, Spangenberg, Dober, David Nitschmann (1740), Anna Nitschmann (Gotha 1740, SPANG 14250-14256).
- The Pennsylvania synods of 1742 (Zinzendorf's "Synod of the sects"; CHRON 11130 and 11423; SPANG contents ~1460; the Büdingen Sammlung öffentlicher Reden, 1746): a meeting of Pennsylvania German churches under Zinzendorf. Library: lamech-agrippa_chronicon-ephratense_hark1889, zinzendorf_sammlung-oeffentlicher-reden-pennsylvania-deu_buedingen1746. Participants: Zinzendorf, Beissel's Ephrata (Chronicon content note).
- Hungarian diets of 1681 (Sopron) and 1790-91 (Pozsony), which the census describes as restoring churches and granting self-government to the Protestants; Peace of Szathmar, 10 May 1711 (a treaty, not a church assembly). No Library file holds them; they are diets, not councils.
- Not a lead: the Diet of Warsaw of 1658, the Second Synod of Utrecht (1763; the Latin title in ACTA 1 reads "secundae Synodi Provinciae Ultrajectensis", so the "Second" is the file's own wording) and the Synod of Pistoia are already on the list.

**Port-Royal, Quietists, Camisards, Huguenots, the French and Roman Catholic church, the Jesuits**

| Assembly | Date | Library file held |
|---|---|---|
| Conferences of Issy (Bossuet, Noailles, Tronson and Fénelon; the thirty-four Issy articles) | 1694-1695, Issy | FEN-M 2 and 8 (the articles printed with the Maxims, English of 1698) |
| Assembly of the clergy of France and the Declaration of the four Gallican articles | 19 March 1682, Paris | ANDERSON 18972 (the Declaration of 1682 in the Anderson volume); ANDERSON 743 (contents) |

| National synods of the Desert (the first provincial synod 21 Aug 1715 at a quarry near Le Vigan; then the national synods to 1796) | 1715-1796, Languedoc and the Cévennes | SYN1, SYN2, SYN3 (acts 1715-1796); COMBE 306-312. The Councils scan lists the French Reformed national synods of 1559-1660 only. |

**Old Believers, the Russian and Ottoman Orthodox, Georgia, Ruthenian and Romanian Greek Catholics**

- Moscow Council of 1654 (and the follow-up council of 1655-56): the council that approved Nikon's book corrections; named in SUB1 2285, 2441, 3200, 3231 ("на соборЪ 1654 года"). Library file held: subbotin_materialy-dlia-istorii-raskola-v1-dokumenty-rus_1874. Not in the Councils_Scan era 8 rows.
- Synod of Alba Iulia 1701 (and the union act of 7 October 1698): named in the Romanian Greek Catholic census entry ("The synod of 1701"); not held (Nilles is not in cic/texts).
- Electoral synod at Alba Iulia, 4 June 1728, called by Charles VI to give three candidates for the bishop's seat: census text; not held.
- Synod of Constantinople of 1755 on the re-baptism of Latin converts: general Orthodox history, not named in any census entry; not searched.
- Georgian synod of 1755 that gave Antoni I the choice to leave or resign: named in the Georgian census entry (Ioselian); not held (Brosset covers the exile only in notes, BROS2 14015-14018).
- Uzhhorod union (1646, confirmed 1652): an assembly of clergy; census text. Library: BASIL 4066-4125 prints the 1652 petition in part. Conversions and unions are stories, not councils; listed only because the 1652 petition is a clergy assembly's act.

**Armenians, Melkites, Copts, Ethiopia, Church of the East, St Thomas Christians, Balkan Catholics**

- National synod of the Melkite Catholics, 25 April 1730 (confirmed Cyril VI's election): MANSI46 546-548 (contents), text of the synod from 187-190 in the printed pages. Also Synod of Saint Isaiah, 31 Jul - 4 Aug 1761 (MANSI46 1434-1436, under the Dominican delegate Dominic de Lanceis); other national synods are listed in MANSI46 935-946, 1596-1619, 1730-1767, 1899-1921 (dates not read). Library file held: MANSI46. (The Synod of Mount Lebanon, 1736, is already on the list.)
- Assembly of Syrian Christians at Mattancherry (the Coonan Cross oath), January 1653, and the gathering at Alengad, 3 January 1654, where Archdeacon Thoma was named Mar Thoma: WHITEHOUSE 19171-19190. The Diamper row mentions the revolt but not these assemblies.
- Assembly of eighty-four churches at Angamaly, 1 February 1787 (the paper "we will have no Bishop but from among ourselves"): WHITEHOUSE 19405-19410 (text printed in Appendix E); not in the councils list.
- Assembly of the fathers of the Bosnian province at Požega, 1 January 1689: FERMENDZIN 24874-24885. A provincial chapter and a declaration of a province, not a council in the narrow sense; offered for the list.
- Assembly on the plain of Mughan, February 1736, which asked Nadir Shah to rule Persia, with Abraham of Crete as one of three Armenian speakers: CHAMICH 22376-22382; ZAKARIA 14292. A political assembly; offered as a summit.
- Council of Ethiopian clergy under the reign of David that tried the Catholic priests and their interpreter (stoned behind the church of Abbo): described in the census from Bruce. No Library file (Bruce is not held).

**Missions: Kongo, Latin America, Philippines, China, Vietnam, Ceylon, the VOC world**

- Synod of Tonkin, 1670, held by Lambert de la Motte; accepted by Clement X in 1673 (census, vietnamese-catholicism-to-1815). Not in Councils_Scan.md. Library file: none held. Launay, Histoire générale de la Société des Missions-Étrangères, vol. 1, would carry it.
- Conference of the missionaries at Canton, 1667-1670, where Jesuit and Dominican exiles drew up common rules for the Chinese rites [recollection; to confirm]. Not in the list. Library file: none known.
- Fourth Mexican Provincial Council, 1771 [recollection; to confirm]. Not in the list. Library file: none known.

**Carried from the era 7 scan**

- Coonan Cross oath at Mattancherry (3 January 1653, per the census documentedStories of st-thomas-christians-under-portuguese-pressure-d; WH 9990-9993, 10028-10036) and the assembly at Allungada (Alangad) that nominated the Archdeacon (WH 9995-10012). These are an oath and an assembly; probably stories rather than councils. Held: WH, SEB1 660-730.
- Assemblies of the churches at Rapolino and Mangate (SEB1 683-690; SEB1 header lines 9-10). Same note: may be treated as part of the 1653 story.
- Angamaly assembly of 1 February 1787, eighty-four churches (WH 19283-19383; census text of st-thomas-christians-two-obediences). Not in Councils_Scan.md. Library file held (Whitehouse App. E).
- National Synod of Loudun (French Reformed), named at LEG2 21630-21636 and LEG2 21787; the year was not read in this file (about 1659-1660 [recollection; to confirm]). Not in Councils_Scan.md. Léger took part through his colleagues' deputations.
- Synod of Dauphiné (LEG2 21630-21636 and 21939-21951) and the Walloon synod at which Léger appeared (LEG2 27169). Regional; probably not major.
- Colloquy of Toruń, 1645 (LUB 115-117): era 7, before the era 8 window, listed for the era 7 scan.

**Sent from the era 9 scan**

- Universalist General Convention at Winchester, New Hampshire, 22 Sep 1803. It adopted the three-article Profession of Belief. Library: CENT 505-575 and 1044-1125 (the convention, its committee Streeter, Richards, Ballou, Ferriss, Lathe; Streeter moderator; Turner and Murray clerks). Assembly, Universalist. Not in the Councils_Scan era 8 rows.
- Universalist Convention at Philadelphia, 1790. It adopted a five-article platform that the 1803 Profession drew on. Library: CENT 1066-1100 (the 1790 articles printed). Recurring body.
- Universalist General Convention of 17 Sep 1807, which ordered the 1808 hymn book. Library: HYM 205-210. Recurring body; probably folded into the General Convention row.
- Synod of Constantinople under Patriarch Sophronios II, 1776, which condemned the Kollyvades and deposed Athanasios Parios (and the 1772 patriarchal synod on the memorial days). Source: census text of the-hesychast-revival-paisius-and-the-philokalia; no Library file identified.
- Herrnhut synod of 1789 (census text of the-moravian-church-at-herrnhut, renewing the Cape plan). Library file not identified in this pass.

## Flagged for overlap with era 7 and era 9

**English-speaking revivals: Quakers, Methodists, New England, Wales, Shakers, New Sweden**

- James Arminius (1560 to 1609), Works vol. 1 (ARM): main work before 1650, era 7. Carried on the Methodist shelf as an antecedent.
- Richard Allen, the AME (founded 1816) and Bethel (1794): scored in era 8; the AME founding is era 9 by one year.
- Jarena Lee (b. 1783), Religious Experience and Journal (1836); call to preach and Allen's permission 1819: main work era 9, not scored here.
- Mary M. Dyer (the Shaker apostate), Brief Statement (1818): era 9; also a namesake trap (see Not scored).
- John Dunlavy, Manifesto (1818): main work after 1815, era 9; he entered the Shakers in the Kentucky revival of 1805. Not scored.
- Frederick W. Evans, Compendium (1859) and Roxalana Grosvenor, Shakers' Covenant (1873): era 9.
- John Hughes, Methodistiaeth Cymru (1851): era 9.
- N. Cynhafal Jones (editor, 1887, 1891), G. R. G. Pughe (translator, 1900), Joseph Morris (1854): later editors, not Whos.
- Sereno Dwight (1830), Thomas Jackson (1865), Curnock (1909), Clay (1835), Burr (1890), Reynolds (1874): later editors and historians, era 9 or after.
- Lemuel Haynes: main work 1776 to the 1820s, straddles; WANTED row above.
- Heckewelder (1820) and Loskiel (1794, Moravian mission history): Loskiel is a Moravian-entry author on the Awakening shelf; not scored here.

**Baptists, Old Dissent, Unitarians and Universalists, Church of England, Swedenborgians, Mennonites, the Cape**

Era 7 (main work before 1650):
- John Smyth, 1609-1612 (founder of the English Baptists; works in smyth_works-v2_whitley1915; held under the-baptists and the-anabaptist-movements).
- Thomas Helwys, 1612-1616 (Mystery of Iniquity, 1612; census voice); the Helwys-congregation pleas of 1615 and 1620 are held as early-baptists_persecution-for-religion... (reprint 1662, authorship to be ruled).
- Roger Williams (Providence, 1636; named in the Baptists legacy).
- Dorothy Hazzard and the Broadmead gathering of 1640 (BROAD 4266, 4314, 4465); Edward Terrill's own work is scored here.
- Faustus Socinus (to 1604) and Samuel Przypkowski (Life written 1636); held under anti-trinitarian currents.
- Menno Simons, Dirk Philips, Hubmaier, Riedemann (the-anabaptist-movements).
- Bunyan's boyhood and army service (before 1650); Baxter's early ministry at Kidderminster (1641 on) fall within the same life and are not split.
- Westminster Assembly 1643-1649 (listed as a council already, era 7).

Era 9 (main work after 1815):
- William Ellery Channing (1819 sermon, channing_sermon... Baltimore 1819).
- Hosea Ballou (main work 1805-1852; scored LISTED here, to be revisited).
- Samuel Marsden (1794-1838; Life by J. B. Marsden 1913 held; most of his work is in New South Wales and New Zealand after 1815).
- John McCarter (The Dutch Reformed Church in South Africa, 1869), Andrew Murray the younger (summoned as moderator, 1865) and T. F. Burgers (suspended, case heard 26 May 1865); the Cape church's first synod of 1824 and everything after 1815.
- Gerhard Wiebe (Winnipeg, 1900), Josef Beck (editor, Vienna 1883), Joseph F. Sohm (translator, 1886 English Martyrs Mirror), the Elkhart manual of 1890 (anonymous), W. T. Whitley (editor, 1909-1910), E. B. Underhill (editor, 1847, 1854): editors and historians of the nineteenth and twentieth centuries.
- William Carey: India 1793-1834 (scored WANTED; main work straddles).
- William Wilberforce: abolition 1807-1833 (WANTED; straddles).
- John Hargrove: the 1816 resurrection sermon (the 1803 sermon is era 8).
- Hannah More and the Cheap Repository (1795-98) are era 8; her later work to 1833 is era 9.

**Pietists, Brethren, Moravians, Hungarian and Transylvanian Protestants, Utrecht**

- Johann Arndt (1555-1621): True Christianity, 1605-10 (ARNDT, German Pietism and Moravian maps, role antecedent). Era 7. Not scored here.
- Jan Amos Comenius (1592-1670): Historia Fratrum Bohemorum and the Labyrinth (COM, Moravian map, role antecedent). Main work 1620s to 1649; era 7. Not scored.
- Jan Lasicki (c.1534-c.1599): Obraz Jednoty (LAS, Moravian map, antecedent). Era 7.
- Józef Ferencz (József Ferencz, b. 1835): Unitárius kis tükör, 1875, and the Short Account of 1907 compiled under him; bishop from 1876 (FERC75, FERC07). Main work 1875: era 9. The census names him as voice 0.
- János Kriza (1811-1875): elected bishop 2 Jul 1861 (FERC75 1318-1319); founded the Keresztény Magvető (MAG61, 1861). Main work 1860s: era 9.
- Pál Augustinovics: bequest of 1837 (FERC75 1227-1230). Era 9. Also Sándor Székely (d. 1852), Sándor Bölöni Farkas (journey of 1831), Dall, Hale and Morison, Channing: all era 9.
- Thomas Rees (1777-1864): Racovian Catechism introduction, 1818 (file racovian-catechism_racovian-catechism_rees1818). Era 9 by date of work. Namesake trap: W. J. Rees, Lives of the Cambro-British Saints (1853).
- John Heckewelder (1743-1823): Narrative of the Mission, 1820 (HECK). Main writing 1818-20, though his mission work runs from 1762: era 9 by date of main work. Flag only.
- Johann Christian Friedrich Burk, Heinrich Guericke and Samuel Jackson (Francke's English translator): authors of 1831-1838 lives; later retellers, not scored.

## Not scored

**English-speaking revivals: Quakers, Methodists, New England, Wales, Shakers, New Sweden**

Groups (not scored):
- The Quaker Great Book of Sufferings (anonymous record-keeping); the "dying sayings" of ordinary Friends in Piety Promoted (FIELD); the Society's yearly-meeting epistles and minutes as a body (LYM, PYM).
- The 24 Swedish pastors sent from 1696 to 1786 (census legacy) and the twenty-five men who ruled against Bruzelius.
- The Arminian Magazine lay narratives (ARMM); the early Methodist preachers other than John Nelson in the Jackson volume: Christopher Hopper, Thomas Mitchell, Peter Jaco, John Haime (PREACH contents lines 81-96). See Question 5.
- The Shaker eight who sailed in 1774 as a body; the Shaker hymn composers of Millennial Praises ("composed by the Believers of different places").
- The Welsh hundred preachers of Rowland (ROW3 128-142) and the Trevecka family.

Namesake traps:
- Mary Dyer: the Quaker martyr hanged at Boston in 1660 (not held) is not the Mary Dyer (Mary M. Dyer) whose 1818 petition against the Enfield Shakers is held (DYER 8). Do not attach the held file to the martyr.
- John Hughes: the author of Methodistiaeth Cymru (HUGH 2 gives "Liverpool (1775-1854, per the host)") is not John Hughes of Pontrobert (the man who wrote down Ann Griffiths's hymns). The dates 1775 to 1854 belong to the Pontrobert Hughes, I believe [recollection; to confirm]; the Liverpool historian is a different man.
- Thomas Jones: the Cofiant compiler is a Welsh Calvinistic Methodist minister (CHAR 8); a second Thomas Jones, the Anglican evangelical of Creaton, is also associated with Charles's circle [recollection; to confirm].
- Samuel Hopkins: the Newport pastor (1721 to 1803, OSB 2) compiled Osborn's Memoirs; Samuel Hopkins of West Springfield (1693 to 1755) is a different man [recollection; to confirm].
- Thomas Prince: the held Christian History names Thomas Prince, junior (1722 to 1748, CHRH 2). The better-known Thomas Prince, senior (1687 to 1758), is a different man [recollection; to confirm].
- Lee: Ann Lee (Shakers), Jesse Lee (Methodist historian) and Jarena Lee (AME) are three different people on these shelves; William Lee is Ann's brother.
- Thomas Brown of Cornwall, New York (BROWN 2) and James Smith (Col.) are common names; no link to other Browns or Smiths in the Library.
- Charles Chauncy of Boston (CHAU 2) and Maurice Chauncy, the Carthusian of the Library (a separate file): different men.
- George Fox (the Quaker, FOX1 1398: Penn's preface says "distinguished from another of that name") is not the Foxe of Acts and Monuments.
- Peter Bohler and Zinzendorf: both are also in the Moravian entry; avoid double rows.
- John Fletcher of Madeley (Mary's husband, an author and Wesley's intended successor) is named in MFL 1947, 3299 but has no file of his own; not scored. WANTED would be Moore's Life of him [recollection; to confirm].

Antecedent-role authors carried on my shelves but owned by other entries, not scored here: William Law (A Serious Call, 1729), Isaac Watts (Hymns and Spiritual Songs, 1725; Psalms of David Imitated, 1719), A. H. Francke, J. A. Bengel (with Burk's 1837 memoir), Count Zinzendorf, the Moravian Church hymnals, Isaac Backus (Appeal, 1773; a Baptist-entry author), Loskiel. Law's and Watts's main work falls in era 8, so a Pietism or Dissent scan should score them; the coordinator should dedupe.

Already scored in earlier eras: none of my candidates appears in the audits of eras 1 to 6 (searched for the names Whitefield, Wesley, Fox, Woolman, Ann Lee, Edwards, Harris).

Host-catalogue attributions only (no line in the text): Rufus Bishop and Seth Youngs Wells (Testimonies), Calvin Green (Summary View), Seth Youngs Wells (Millennial Praises). Not scored.

**Port-Royal, Quietists, Camisards, Huguenots, the French and Roman Catholic church, the Jesuits**

- Groups and collectives: the sixteen Carmelites of Compiègne (martyred 17 July 1794); the solitaires of Port-Royal beyond those named (d'Andilly, Le Maître, Sacy and others); the nuns who signed or refused the Formulary; the Camisard bands and child prophets (the "twenty young boys" and others); the deponents of Misson's collection other than the named; the women prisoners of the Tour de Constance other than Marie Durand; Court's first colleagues of 1715 (Bonbonnoux, Rouvière, Corteiz, Brunel; COMBE 300-305); the Desert pastors and synods as bodies; the jurors and refractory clergy; the Jesuits of New France (authors of the Jesuit Relations vols 36-40, a corporate series) and the Jesuit letter-writers of the Mémoires de la Chine (tomes 19, 20, 22); the Quebec bishops after Laval; the Carthusian general chapter (statutes of 1681); the Roman Missal; the Council of Trent; the Church of Utrecht's Recueil de divers témoignages (1763).
- Modern scholars and editors: Lowndes, Coquerel, Combe, Hugues, Sagnier, Richaudeau, Têtu and Gagnon, Gazier, Neale, Faber, Pollen, Anderson, Stewart, M'Crie, Allen, Marston.
- Rulers and powers named as forces (Question 2): Napoleon, Paul I, Louis XV, Joseph II, Madame de Maintenon, the Catholic crowns of France, Spain, Portugal, Naples and Sicily.
- Not handled here: Gilbert Burnet (Anglican bishop of Salisbury; his Letter I on Molinos is on the Quietist shelf). He belongs to the Restoration and Georgian Church of England scan; if that scan does not take him, he is LISTED as a rival-church traveller and not an outside witness.
- Namesake traps: Jean Cavalier the commander and Jean Cavalier of Sauve, his cousin, a deponent in Misson (MISS 3831-3834); the three Angéliques of Port-Royal (Marie Angélique Arnauld, Angélique de Saint-Jean, Marie Angélique de Sainte-Thérèse, PRD 25707); the Rabauts (Paul Rabaut, Rabaut Saint-Étienne, Rabaut-Pomier, Rabaut-Dupuis, Jacques Rabaut a pastor at the synods, SYN3 651); Antoine Court and Antoine Court de Gébelin (the Paris scholar to whom Rabaut wrote, COQ2 15420); Marie de l'Incarnation of Quebec and the sister of Compiègne; François Noël, S.J. and Noël Negabamat; Clement XI and Clement XIV; the spurious "Lettres de Ganganelli"; Innocent X, XI and XII; Fontaine the Port-Royal secretary and James Fontaine the refugee minister.

Library label errors and odd editions:

- TEST: the file is the second edition of 1888, not the 1816 Hancock first edition the census names; the title page names no compiler.

- The Jesse Lee file and Coke file are held under the Methodist shelf; the Quaker shelf's cross-link rows ("outside witness in passing") use provisional confidence.
- Jarena Lee's file (1836) sits on the Methodist shelf but belongs to era 9.

**Pietists, Brethren, Moravians, Hungarian and Transylvanian Protestants, Utrecht**

WANTED sources:
- Johanna Eleonora Petersen: her Leben (1718), the census's own source; the Library's note (JWP 9) says it is not held. The Becker-Cantarino English (Chicago 2005) is modern and copyrighted; an early German print is the need [suggested; to confirm].
- Gottfried Arnold: Unparteiische Kirchen- und Ketzer-Historie (1699-1700), the Frankfurt edition of 1729 [suggested; to confirm].
- Rebecca Protten: her letters and Lebenslauf from the Unity Archives, in a published edition [suggested; to confirm].
- Christian David's own Beschreibung (1735) [suggested; to confirm]; Anna Nitschmann's Lebenslauf [suggested; to confirm].
- Hungarian Protestantism 1650-1815: Bauhofer, History of the Protestant Church in Hungary (tr. Craig, Boston 1854) and Aldasy, "Hungary", Catholic Encyclopedia vol. 7, both named by the census as "read raw online" but not in cic/texts; a named source for the galley pastors of 1676.
- Tranquebar: Fenger, History of the Tranquebar Mission (1863) and an early Halle report on Aaron's ordination, 1733 [suggested; to confirm].
- Szentábrahámi's Christian Dogmatic and the Generale Protocollum 1626-1737, cited in MAG61 7014 and 7327-7340 [suggested; to confirm]. Varlet's Apologies [recollection; to confirm].
- The Hallesche Berichte (Halle reports) and the Gemein-Diarium of Herrnhut, named by the census, are not held.

Library label errors and odd editions:
- lamech-agrippa_chronicon-ephratense_hark1889, header line 2: names "Brother Lamech (d. 1763) and 'Agrippa' (Johann Peter Miller, 1710-1796)", but the translator's preface (CHRON 160-168) says the authors' identity is "utterly unknown".
- mack-alexander_writings-bilingual_columbiana1860, header line 2: attributes the memoir to "Alexander Mack Jr. (1712-1803)". The text shows the memoir is by the 1860 editor, who says he gathered few materials (MACK 298-303, 365-379).
- church-of-utrecht_recueil-de-divers-temoignages-fra_1763 line 26493: Steenoven's letter to Noailles is dated in the OCR "4 Décembre 1714"; an archbishop-elect of 1723-24 cannot write this in 1714, so the year is probably 1724 in the scan [to confirm against the page image]. The Recueil also names Barchman's letters to Varlet.
- corpus-map hoynck-van-papendrecht_historia-ecclesiae-ultrajectinae-lat_1725 is marked "context" but is the case against the Utrecht chapter (header and content note): a polemic of the opposing party.
- church-of-utrecht_acta-et-decreta-secundae-synodi-lat_1764: the title reads "secundae Synodi Provinciae Ultrajectensis" (the Councils scan asks whether "Second" is right): it is the file's own wording, which counts the 1763 synod as the second provincial synod.
- cennick_life-of-mr-j-cennick_1745: the corpus-map puts it under the Moravians; the "Brethren" of his 1739 text (CENN 1883-1897) are the Bristol and London societies and do not clearly mean the Moravians. Check against the Library thread.
- hungarian-and-transylvanian-protestantism-1650-1815: no corpus-map file exists.
- Transylvanian Unitarian Church census entry cites József Ferencz's 1875 book as "primary"; it is a nineteenth-century retelling for the 1660-1815 events.

**Port-Royal, Quietists, Camisards, Huguenots, the French and Roman Catholic church, the Jesuits**

WANTED sources, with editions tagged:
- Antoine Arnauld: Œuvres (Lausanne, 1775-83) [suggested; to confirm]; the Port-Royal Logic (1662) [suggested; to confirm].
- Pasquier Quesnel: Réflexions morales (1692-94) [suggested; to confirm]; his correspondence [suggested; to confirm].
- Marie Durand: her letters from the Tour de Constance; edition not named [suggested; to confirm]. COQ1 21811 says many exist.
- Abraham Mazel: his own mémoires; edition not named [suggested; to confirm].
- Muratori: Soli Muratori's Vita (Venice, 1756) [suggested; to confirm]; Il cristianesimo felice (1743), online at archive.org A118051 per the census.
- Rancé: Marsollier's Vie (1703) [suggested; to confirm].
- Fontaine and du Fossé: the Mémoires (Cologne, 1738; Utrecht, 1739) [suggested; to confirm].
- Wanted outside witnesses for these entries (no rows): Voltaire (Traité sur la tolérance, 1763; Siècle de Louis XIV), Rulhière (Éclaircissements historiques sur la révocation de l'édit de Nantes, 1788) [suggested; to confirm].

Library label and edition issues:
- RANCE1 (and the t2 volume): the title page names no author; the host record's attribution to Rancé stands alone. Check before using it as his work.
- The corpus-map's quietist shelf lists the Burnet letter as "an outside Protestant witness". Burnet is an Anglican bishop; the label "outside witness" should be dropped from that note.
- The corpus-map's Camisard shelf file MISS (Bost 1847) content note lists Abraham Mazel among the deponents; no deposition under his name was found in the held lines (grep Mazel: mentions only). Misson's "Jean Cavalier" deponent is the cousin of the commander (MISS 3831-3834); the shelf note does not say so.
- The corpus-map's Desert shelf says the prisoners' letters survive "in fragments" in Sagnier; the held lines of SAG print register entries and a few letters of Paul Rabaut, not letters of the prisoners.
- MINC2 note: "the tree has no New France entry after 1650"; the era 7 New France entry (1610-1650) ends there, so the shelf is right but the Laval and Marie rows are provisional.
- The Mirbt file's no. 454 (MIRBT 22854-22862) is Benedict XIV's bull of 20 Dec 1741; the entry uses only that number.
- Councils list gap: the Desert synods of 1715-1796, the Assembly of 1682 and the Conferences of Issy are not on the Councils list (see Leads).

**Old Believers, the Russian and Ottoman Orthodox, Georgia, Ruthenian and Romanian Greek Catholics**

WANTED sources:
- Nicodemus the Hagiorite: a Life (Hieromonk Eutymios of Athos, Life of Nicodemus the Hagiorite) [suggested; to confirm]; also A Handbook of Spiritual Counsel (CWS 1989) and Concerning Frequent Communion (with Macarius), both named by the census [suggested editions; to confirm].
- Tikhon of Zadonsk: a life by his cell-attendant Chebotarev [suggested; to confirm], plus Of True Christianity [suggested; to confirm].
- Herman of Alaska: Little Russian Philokalia vol. 3, Saint Herman (Platina 1971) and Hieromonk Gideon's voyage journal [both suggested; to confirm].
- Klein: Bunea, Episcopul Ioan Inocenţiu Klein (Blaj 1900) and Nilles, Symbolae (Innsbruck 1885-86), both named in the census (read online, not vendored). Question 2.
- Stefan Yavorsky (LISTED, would become WANTED): The Rock of Faith (Kamen' very, 1728) [suggested; to confirm].
- Antoni I of Georgia (STORY, would be WHO): his translations and grammar [suggested; to confirm]; Ioselian (Malan 1866) and Dowling (1912) are named in the census but not vendored.
- Semyon Denisov: Vinograd Rossiiskii and his history of the Solovki fathers (the census names them; only a Barskov quotation is held).
- Dositheus II: his Dodekabiblos (History of the Patriarchs of Jerusalem, 1715) [suggested; to confirm].
- Macarius III Zaim: Paul's journal is held; the Arabic original is not.

Library label errors and odd editions:
- Census says Macrina (Georgia) is sister of King Teimuraz I; Brosset (BROS2 26582-26588, 30763-30766) has Mariam, nun Macriné, as sister of King David (Imam-Quli-Khan, d. 1722). Check the census text against Ioselian.
- Census text calls Dositheus "II"; the Library's Synod files do not number him.
- The Gedeon heading (GED 11937-11939) names "Gregory" with no number; the "V" is the census's inference. Confirm that the 1797 encyclical is Gregory V's by the date on the page.
- SREG-E (Consett 1729) is read through the long s; line numbers from the Russian 1856 edition are used for the Regulation signatories.
- Harrison and Mirrlees' preface gives April 1681 for the burning of Avvakum; the census gives 1682. Contested.
- AVV-W (Wikisource) carries 116 bracketed modern glosses (corpus-map note); quote from AVV-K or HM when a verbatim check is wanted.
- PH1 and PED are the Athens 1893 and 1886 reprints, not the Venice 1782 and Leipzig 1800 originals (corpus-map note); the OCR Greek is accent-garbled.
- BROS2 is Brosset's French translation of the Georgian chronicles (1857), a later translator's text, not the chronicles; cite accordingly.
- Dates in rows marked "not in an opened line" come from the drafter's general knowledge, not from a Library line. The checker should confirm or downgrade them.

- WANTED sources:
  - James Bruce, Travels to Discover the Source of the Nile, vols. 2 and 3 (Edinburgh, 1790), named in the census, not held.
  - For Ibrahim al-Jawhari: al-Jabarti, Aja'ib al-athar [suggested; to confirm]; Butcher, Story of the Church of Egypt, vol. 2 (1897), named in the census, not held. Al-Jabarti would also be a candidate Who (OUTSIDE WITNESS) on his own, as a Muslim chronicler of Egypt in this window [suggested; to confirm].
- Census sources named "read online" but not vendored: Volney vol. 1 (Egypt), Lane (1860), Butcher, Gobat (1834), Bruce vols. 2 and 3, Fortescue "Melchites", Labourt "Chaldean Christians", Catholic Encyclopedia "Abyssinia", Buchanan is held, Whitehouse is held.
- Charon is held for tomes II and III only (CHARON 1-3); tome I, which the text cites for the 1724 split and Euthymios Saifi's liturgical reforms (CHARON 32282-32284), is not held.
- Corpus-map is missing for five census ids: armenian-apostolic-church-era-8, the-coptic-church-under-the-ottomans, ethiopian-christianity-gondar-to-the-zemene-mesafint, church-of-the-east-three-patriarchates, catholics-of-the-ottoman-balkans. Armenian Apostolic also has `sources: null` in the census.

- Census quotation for Gomidas not found in the Library (see Calls). Census claim that Ephraim came to Etchmiadzin in 1810 under Russian guard not found in a held line.
- Lead for a Bosnian Franciscan source of the 1700s (a province chronicle) to pair with Fermendzin's Latin calendar [suggested; to confirm]; the census itself says the Library calendar stops at 1752 and shows no Catholic voice of the people.
- Library label: the Mansi 46 header says "Era VIII pass"; Brosset's volume (ZAKARIA header) is catalogued as "1886" on the host, correct year 1876; a note only.

**Missions: Kongo, Latin America, Philippines, China, Vietnam, Ceylon, the VOC world**

WANTED sources:
- Capuchin relations on Kimpa Vita (Bernardo da Gallo; Lorenzo da Lucca), named in the census; Brásio, Monumenta Missionaria Africana for Portuguese-side documents [suggested; to confirm the volumes]. One item behind three rows (Kimpa Vita, da Gallo, Pedro IV). Thornton 1998 is a later retelling and does not replace them.
- Úrsula de Jesús, The Souls of Purgatory, ed. Nancy van Deusen (UNM Press, 2004). Rights likely not clear; the Library should check, and look for the Spanish edition.
- Vieira: Maria Rosa Mystica (Lisbon, 1686), named in the census; André de Barros, Vida do apostolico padre Antonio Vieira (Lisbon, 1746), and the Cartas [suggested; to confirm].
- Maigrot's Mandate of 1693; Launay, Histoire générale de la Société des Missions-Étrangères, vol. 1 (Paris, 1894) [named in the census].
- Pallu and Lambert de la Motte: their Relations and letters; the Monita [suggested; to confirm].
- Rhodes: Divers voyages et missions (Paris, 1653) [suggested; to confirm]; Catechismus (Rome, 1651), named in the census.
- Philiphê Bỉnh: his own writings; Dutton, A Vietnamese Moses (UC Press, 2017), open access, named in the census.
- Joseph Vaz: Sebastião do Rego, Vida do venerável padre Joseph Vaz (1744) [suggested; to confirm]; the Sketch of the Rise and Progress of the Catholic Church in Ceylon (Colombo, 1848), census source, not held.
- Baldaeus: Naauwkeurige beschryvinge van Malabar en Choromandel ... Ceylon (Amsterdam, 1672) [suggested; to confirm].
- For Almeida, Leijdecker and Werndly: an early life source such as Valentijn's Oud en Nieuw Oost-Indiën [suggested; to confirm]; see Question 3.

Library label errors and odd editions:
- PAIVA header dates the pope's letter to "Annae, reginae Singae" 7 Feb 1665, though other sources place Njinga's death in Dec 1663 [recollection; to confirm]. Check the heading and the letter's addressee.
- CAV is "compiled and arranged" by Alamandini (CAV 2); the corpus-map note calls it Cavazzi's history. Say so in the cell where the voice is quoted.
- ZUC and MER are a German translation and an edited Italian; cite as the translator's or editor's wording.
- The corpus-map has no file for protestant-churches-of-the-voc-world, vietnamese-catholicism-to-1815 or catholics-of-dutch-ceylon; the census names sources for all three (Aritonang and Steenbrink, Launay, Dutton, the 1848 Sketch) that are not in cic/texts.
- The Sketch of 1848 (census: "A Catholic account", archive.org attributes it to Simon Casie Chitty): check the attribution and the church of the author [recollection: a Ceylon Tamil Christian; to confirm].
- BARBOSA 13641-13645 is the only held mention of Rhodes: an editor's note, not Rhodes.
- BR48 3880-3925 holds a Philippine Dominican account of the Tonkin "Lovers of the Cross" dispute (1751-65): a lead for the Vietnamese entry.
- LAUNAY-K is the Kouy-tcheou volume (1907), not the Société des Missions-Étrangères vol. 1 (1894) the census names; the corpus-map labels it "naming its Chinese priests".

**Carried from the era 7 scan**

- Archdeacon Thomas: no source in the Library tells his life from inside. The community's text is a brief history and a paper of 1787. A better one would be a Malankara or Syriac memoir of Mar Thoma I; none is known [suggested; to confirm: the Jesuit archbishop Garcia's letters of 1653-1654, summarized in Thekkedath (census), for a second hostile witness].
- Brandt: volumes 1, 2 and 4 are not held; the dates of his birth and death are not in any held line. Suggested: the Dutch text of the Historie der Reformatie, or Loeff's Life of Brandt (Utrecht, 1864, cited at TID 6281) [suggested; to confirm].
- Lobo: the held text is Johnson's abridged English (1735) of Le Grand's French. The Portuguese manuscript (Lisbon, St Roque) is not held. Suggested: a modern edition of the Portuguese Itinerário [suggested; to confirm].
- Léger: the year of his birth is unreadable in the scan (LEG2 29760). A cleaner scan or Léger's life in a reference work would settle it.
- Lubieniecki: the printed death year reads "1475" in the Vita (LUB 500); the Library header should note this as a print or OCR slip for 1675.
- Odd editions: SEB1 and SEB2 are rough OCR (word share 0.77 to 0.79, per the headers), so every quote above is OCR as printed and must be checked on the page image. VIN is also rough OCR (0.83). MOR is a Princeton copy lacking two pages (header line 9). BRA3 is volume 3 only, the English of 1722, microfilm OCR. The Library header for LOBO says the Gutenberg text was "prepared from the 1887 Cassell edition", and the introduction in it is the 1887 editor's, not Lobo's.
- Library label check: corpus-map files the Sebastiani and Vincenzo Maria rows under church-of-the-east-schism-of-1552.yaml as "context" on the overland route; the Malabar material (SEB1 Libro Secondo, SEB2 Libro Secondo, VIN Libro Secondo) belongs to the two St Thomas Christian entries and is not mapped to them there.
